@@139268977 <h> Indigenous rights and Pine Gap <p> Overhead , as Arrernte Elder Felicity Hayes hands a large , printed letter to an Australian Federal Police inspector at the gates of Pine Gap , there is the loud buzz of a drone . <p> Hayes 's family are the Traditional Owners of Kuyunba , land occupied by the Joint Defence Facility for 60 years . Three generations of Hayeses stand before one of the United States 's most strategically important military bases , just outside Alice Springs . Behind the high and closely surveilled perimeter lie a number of sacred sites , some inaccessible for decades . <p> About 100 metres back , behind a police line , are hundreds of protesters who have come from across the country to support the Hayes family 's demands to close the facility and return the land to First Nations custodians . This is the largest action taken against Pine Gap since 1987 . <p> The protest was the finale to a three-day-long " Close Pine Gap " convergence , organised by activist groups Mparntwe for Falastin and the Anti-Militarism Action Network . The @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ gather for panels and workshops discussing resistance to militarisation and colonialism . <p> " It 's a really compelling geopolitical moment , " says Jorgen Doyle , an organiser of the convergence , " to contest Pine Gap and everything that it represents . " <p> The base at Pine Gap collects information from multiple satellites located above the Indian Ocean , which are able to gather large volumes of data from the Middle East , Europe and Africa . <p> While activity at the base is highly secretive , multiple leaked National Security Agency documents published by Background Briefing in 2017 confirmed that the United States can use Pine Gap 's intelligence to support its military operations , including geolocation used for lethal targeting action . <p> Hayes 's letter , handed to the AFP , addressed these capabilities and their potential uses . <p> " We feel sorry for the little ones , " it says , " the families , men , women , children , old people overseas in Gaza and other places who are getting killed by that base . " <p> A short drive @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , many of the Hayes family are living without basic services . <p> Residents at White Gate camp , originally known as Irrkerlantye , live in a few sheds , mostly made of tin . Their water must be trucked in , there is no sewerage and a sparse power supply is provided by a few solar panels . <p> Hayes says she wishes people could see how her family lives . " The power and the running water goes up to the gun club ... and from there on , there 's nothing . " <p> This lack of resources sits in stark contrast to Australia 's increasing military spending . Over the next two years , $200 million has been committed to establish AUKUS Command -- a specialised arm of the AFP , solely tasked with providing security and protest response at " Specified Defence Bases " . <p> Greens Senator David Shoebridge says that after making inquiries about AUKUS Command 's budget in Senate estimates , he was told " they 're not there to protect the Australian part of the base ... It 's $100 million @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ personnel and their contractors . " <p> Prior to the convergence , attendees said they worried this specialised arm of the AFP might respond to protest action on July 20 with increased powers . <p> " Our struggle is always the same : trying to keep the poisons away from Country , the evils , trying to bring Country back to life , support people , protect people , looking after the next generation . " <p> To the surprise of many , police presence was minimal on the day , with only a handful of AFP officers present at the handover of Felicity Hayes 's letter . However , several AFP officers with large items of checked luggage , marked " AUKUS " and " WC Port " , were seen at the airport , leaving Alice Springs the following morning . <p> Freedom of information documents published this year show Western Command ( WC ) and AUKUS Command are working in partnership towards a permanent AFP presence at HMAS Stirling -- a primary AUKUS base on Garden Island , just south of Perth , undergoing an $8 billion @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> In response to questions about the convergence and AUKUS Command 's possible presence in Alice Springs , the AFP declined to comment . <p> AUKUS was top of mind throughout the three-day protest , as Indigenous survivors of nuclear testing and military expansion noted their own history and Australia 's increasingly militarised future . <p> Karina Lester , a Yankunytjatjara A ? angu woman and second-generation survivor of the British nuclear testing that took place at Emu Field , is seeking clarity around Australia 's role in the agreement . She is serving as a commissioner for the independent public inquiry into AUKUS . <p> " What we are hearing is that there are no criteria or guidelines ... The Commonwealth can come in and pick future locations with very little consultation to First Nations peoples . " <p> For senior Googatha elder and president of the Australian Nuclear Free Alliance Aunty Sue Coleman-Haseldine , impacts of military programs have been pervasive throughout her life . <p> Coleman-Haseldine was two years old when the first atomic bomb tests began north-west of her Country in 1953 . Those tests @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> Decades later , dismissing cautions , she regularly maintains sacred rock holes on her Country , which now sit within Koonibba Test Range , the largest commercial rocket testing facility in the southern hemisphere . <p> When asked how her advocacy relates to Pine Gap , Coleman-Haseldine says : " Our struggle is always the same : trying to keep the poisons away from Country , the evils , trying to bring Country back to life , support people , protect people , looking after the next generation . " <p> Coleman-Haseldine questions whether AUKUS will continue a legacy of damage to people and Country , in Australia and abroad . <p> As with AUKUS , details surrounding Pine Gap remain largely classified . However , decades of investigations and leaked intelligence documents have revealed key aspects of the facility 's role in US military operations globally . <p> A 2013 Fairfax Media investigation confirmed that US drone strikes have used intelligence from Pine Gap to conduct targeted killings of Al Qaeda and Taliban members in Pakistan . These same drone programs were later found to be responsible @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , widely considered Australia 's leading expert on Pine Gap , says Pine Gap 's top tasking priorities would be China , North Korea , the Gaza conflict , including Lebanon and Yemen , and Iran . <p> This potential involvement in major conflicts was a significant motivator for the convergence . <p> While the Australian government refuses to comment on whether Pine Gap is involved in Israel 's bombardment of Gaza , which the United Nations Independent International Commission of Inquiry on the Occupied Palestinian Territory declared a genocide , Tanter believes intelligence sharing is occurring . <p> In March , The Guardian reported that the US has been using Pine Gap to collect and analyse signals to locate military targets across Iran . At the time , a spokesperson responded to questions put to the government by saying the Department of Defence " does not comment " on the operation of its joint facilities , including Pine Gap . <p> Tanter previously found further evidence that linked Pine Gap 's likely surveillance of Iran through openly published personnel requirements for work positions at Pine Gap . Specifically , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the NSA 's operations at the base advertised for a Farsi-speaking US citizen for a classified position to work in Alice Springs with the US military to focus on the Persian Gulf . <p> John Blaxland , professor of international security and intelligence studies at the Australian National University , endorses Pine Gap , AUKUS and other related defence projects . He argues that their collective existence is vital for Australia , especially as China increases its militarisation of north-east Asia and the South China Sea . <p> Blaxland says that if Australia were not to continue with AUKUS and other military projects , it would accentuate our risk of war with China . <p> " This is the bargain , " he says . " We are trying to make war less likely by making the cost too high of risking it . " <p> For Aunty Sue Coleman-Haseldine , however , strategic calculations offer little reassurance . She warns that escalating defence posturing has the very real consequence of steering Australia towards catastrophe , which her community and other First Nations people across the country have experienced firsthand @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ prejudice . It does n't care whether you 're Black , white , brindle , rich , poor or in the middle , " she says . " It 'll still get you . It 's the great equaliser . " <p> Alexandra Nevill is vice-president of the Melbourne University Journalism Association . 
@@139268999 <h> The news division <p> Amid stories of power struggles and personal conflict within the ABC , news director Justin Stevens was unceremoniously pushed out in May . What 's behind the turmoil ? <p> At 1.52pm on Wednesday , May 27 , an all-staff email landed like a grenade in the inboxes of 2000 ABC News staff across Australia . " Dear News colleagues , I write to inform you that after 19 years , including the last four as Director of News , I have resigned from the ABC . " Mobile phone messages pinged around the nation : " WTF ? ! ! " Only a few days earlier , the ABC 's jocular and well-liked news boss Justin Stevens had been in his Ultimo office , in inner Sydney , planning for the months ahead . Then he 'd been called into a meeting with his boss , ABC managing director Hugh Marks , on the seventh floor . Stevens left the building shortly afterwards on unscheduled leave . <p> Barely a minute after Stevens ' bombshell landed , another all-staff email arrived from Marks , informing staff " Justin 's last day at the ABC is today . " There would be no farewell , no gushing tributes from his superiors , just a cursory few pars in recognition of his two decades of service . Marks thanked Stevens for his " incredible commitment " and " outstanding results " and wished him every success . Scarcely another minute later , the news of Stevens ' demise was splashed in the Nine newspapers , the result of either lightning-fast reporting or a tip-off . The Sydney Morning Herald added , " The search for his replacement is already close to completion . " <p> Resigned ? No one believed it . " It looked like what it was -- a serious backstabbing , " says one ABC veteran . <p> The next day , Marks fronted Senate estimates to face a grilling from former ABC staffer turned Coalition senator Sarah Henderson . Had Stevens been pushed or threatened with the sack ? A former Nine chief executive and practised media performer , Marks assured her , straight-faced , " Mr Stevens felt it was the right time for him to move on " . But even as Marks was being questioned , news broke that Reuters executive Simon Robinson would replace Stevens in the role . Marks confirmed that he had been canvassing candidates for the past six months " knowing that Mr Stevens was potentially leaving the organisation " . The next day the ABC affirmed Robinson 's appointment . <p> Stevens ' abrupt departure shook the organisation . " It was a brutal sacking , " says a long-time workmate . " At Channel Nine , that 's business as usual , but not at the ABC . " <p> In an outpouring on social media , colleagues praised Stevens ' decency , kindness , generosity , intelligence and professionalism . He was lauded as a " fierce advocate for investigative public affairs journalism " , " a leader , a gentleman , a thinker " and a boss who showed " it 's possible to lead with heart and integrity even in the most demanding job in Australia " . Close colleague and friend Leigh Sales called it " a big loss for the ABC " , praising her former boss 's " huge brain " and " impeccable integrity " . <p> Privately , some were more sanguine . " I feel for him because it was so brutal , but then I remembered how much money he was earning , " says one . " If you 're taking a massive salary , you die by the sword . If they do n't like what you 're doing , you get knifed , and that 's what happened , " says another . Not everyone was surprised . " The view was Justin was out of his depth . He was n't ready for that role . " <p> On the face of it , the news division under Stevens had been kicking goals . ABC News ranks as the most trusted news brand in Australia . Its online service is the most popular news website . The ratings of its weekday 7pm news bulletin were up 13 per cent over two years , while its nightly flagship show 7.30 was up 12 per cent , a noteworthy achievement at a time when free-to-air television is steadily declining . So why , to use Nine parlance , had Stevens been boned ? <p> In a conspicuous coincidence of timing , in the middle of the same Senate estimates hearing , Marks confirmed an ABC news report that the corporation had sacked Four Corners reporter Mahmood Fazal , a former officeholder of the Mongols outlaw motorcycle gang who was hired by the program as a reporter in 2022 . Fazal had been embroiled in controversy over his dealings with underworld figures and a podcast with a former criminal who 'd gone public to attack him . Marks told the Senate the ABC had investigated the podcast and , as a result , " Fazal is no longer employed by the ABC . " <p> At the end of Marks ' Senate interrogation , there were more questions than answers about Stevens ' ouster . Were there legitimate concerns over his leadership and judgement ? Was he deemed to be not tough enough for the role ? Was it a personality clash between the mild-mannered news chief and his more forceful bosses , Marks and ABC chair Kim Williams ? Was Stevens the loser in a contest of swinging dicks ? Was his downfall connected with Fazal 's ? Were major changes afoot that Stevens was powerless to resist ? Were more heads set to roll ? The answer -- based on conversations with more than 60 current and former ABC staff , and others -- is all of the above . <p> Turmoil at the national broadcaster is nothing new . The ABC has been roiled over decades by political attacks , funding cuts , clashes between powerful personalities , and fights over editorial direction , culture and priorities . <p> Ultimo old-timers still groan into their beers at the Agincourt Hotel over memories of self-declared " mini media mogul " Jonathan Shier strutting about onstage chanting " I 've got the gig ! " after his appointment by the Howard government as managing director in 2000 . His antics convulsed the organisation until he was sacked after 19 months . Fewer still have forgotten the triennium of chaos during Michelle Guthrie 's reign as managing director , which ended in 2018 when she was fired by chairman Justin Milne . Within days , Milne himself was forced to resign after reports that he had urged Guthrie to sack two senior reporters because the government " hated " them . <p> In the wake of the Guthrie -- Milne fiasco , the Morrison government named Ita Buttrose as chair and she appointed ABC loyalist David Anderson as managing director . A veteran of 35 years who started as a teenager changing tea towels in the ABC mailroom , Anderson was a safe pair of hands -- cautious , steadfast , reliable . His job was to steady the ship . But he too was worn down by a hostile government , and his relations with Buttrose grew toxic . Their era also ended in acrimony with the sacking of radio host Antoinette Lattouf , three days into a five-day casual radio stint , after a campaign by pro-Israel lobbyists offended by her support for Gaza , even though she had n't expressed it on air . <p> A Federal Court judge later fined the ABC $220,000 for illegally terminating her contract , ruling : " The ABC let down the Australian public badly when it abjectly surrendered the rights of its employee Ms Lattouf to appease a lobby group . " <p> The ABC 's budget has shrunk by a third in real terms since the 1980s . Financially squeezed and under pressure to compete in an ever more brutal media landscape , it 's undergone a series of restructures and rolling waves of redundancies . From 2013 , a succession of Coalition governments cut funding by $526 million over eight years , causing the loss of 640 jobs . Dozens of senior staff have been made redundant or encouraged to resign , leaving the ranks of experienced journalists depleted . At the same time , the broadcaster 's output has grown exponentially with the launch of ABC News Digital , the 24-hour News Channel , and an explosion of podcasts and social media content . <p> The onslaught from the Murdoch media and conservative antagonists has escalated with the resurgence of One Nation , which recently banned the ABC from a press conference and wants to move it to a subscription model . In June , the right-wing lobby group Advance launched a campaign to " shut down the ABC " , citing what it claims is systemic bias . Journalist and academic Margaret Simons , who is writing a two-volume history of the national broadcaster , says this perennial claim is unfounded . " Conservative criticism is usually over allegations of bias , " Simons says . " Nobody has ever managed to prove bias . Most Australians do n't think the ABC is biased . " <p> " It feels like the ABC is under as much if not more pressure than it has ever been , " says Alan Sunderland , the corporation 's former head of editorial policy . " Much of our public debate and much of our politics has become a polarised culture war ... The ABC and its programming have now become weaponised in that war . Bashing the ABC , and to a lesser extent defending the ABC , have become ways of signalling which side you are on in the culture war . " <p> As a result , the ABC has become unnerved and its staff demoralised , says Sunderland . " The ABC at times has lost the ability to resist outside pressure and stand up for itself . They are sometimes criticised for being slow to admit error and that is sometimes true , but they are also too willing to back down . " He cites the Lattouf saga as a case in point . " It demonstrated the tendency to buckle under pressure . " <p> Into the fray in 2022 stepped young Justin Stevens , taking the job as director of news at the tender age of 38 . Many were startled by his sudden rise . Stevens is the son of a paediatric oncologist and alumnus of the posh St Ignatius ' College Riverview , whose Latin motto Quantum Potes Tantum Aude means " as much as you can do , so much dare to do " . Stevens had been the interview producer for 7.30 stars Kerry O'Brien and Leigh Sales , and a producer at Four Corners . He had produced the documentary series Keating : The Interviews and the Logie-winning three-parter The Killing Season , and had then been executive producer of 7.30 for four years . <p> Stevens ' promoter was the then managing director David Anderson , who backed him over two more seasoned contenders . Anderson and Stevens share similar traits . Like his old boss , Stevens presents as modest , affable , sincere , collegial and down to earth . He dislikes conflict , embraces consultation , prefers to minimise risk . Many considered him young for the role , but he was popular among staff and had the backing of powerful names such as Leigh Sales and Sarah Ferguson . <p> Under Anderson , Stevens launched the ABC 's " digital first " strategy and spearheaded another restructure to shift focus and resources from its " legacy " platforms ( TV and radio ) to online . One hundred and twenty jobs were made redundant , including , controversially , that of political editor Andrew Probyn , who had survived a campaign against him by the former Coalition government only to find himself surplus to the corporation 's needs . <p> The ABC 's news story on Stevens ' departure noted that his tenure was " marked by controversy " . In 2023 , the broadcaster lost a landmark defamation case and had to pay more than $400,000 in damages to former special forces soldier Heston Russell , over a series of stories about alleged war crimes in Afghanistan . In a stinging rebuke to the ABC during this critical test case , the judge rejected its public interest defence , finding the drafting of a key story " did not differentiate between suspicion , allegations and proven facts " . The judge also criticised a " disturbing " press release issued by the ABC , in which Stevens suggested the corporation 's defence had been undermined by its ethical obligation to protect a confidential source . The judge found this was " misleading " , and that it was the ABC 's " own editorial choices " that left its journalists unable to protect the source . <p> There was more uproar over a follow-up story , when it was found a video editor had mistakenly added extra gunshots into a clip posted online . Former editorial policy head Alan Sunderland was brought in to do a review , and found that the gunshots had been added in error . He also found that " the stories contained flaws which in my view undermined their accuracy and impact , and had the potential to mislead audiences ... For a public broadcaster that relies on its reputation for rigorous journalism and seeks the trust of Australians , that is not an acceptable outcome . " Stevens was forced to apologise . <p> Stevens was again embroiled in controversy when veteran broadcaster Stan Grant quit the ABC , accusing the organisation of " institutional failure " for not defending him amid a torrent of public abuse , after he commented during King Charles 's coronation that the British crown represents the invasion and theft of Indigenous land . Grant said he endured " relentless racial filth " and no one at the ABC uttered a word of public support . He pointedly did n't blame Stevens , whom he said had been " a support and comfort " . ( After Stevens ' departure , Grant commented on LinkedIn : " You took on an enormous job and leave an admirable legacy . Most importantly you never lost who you are and leave with your integrity and humanity intact . " ) The ABC launched a review into internal racism and Stevens found himself apologising again : " To anyone in News who has ever experienced any racism or bigotry in our workplace ... I would like to express my sorrow for that and make a heartfelt apology . " <p> Stevens took everything to heart and agonised over the impact of his decisions , according to admirers . " His flaw was caring too much . He was almost too virtuous for that job , " says a supporter . <p> Stevens was also lumped with the fallout from Lattouf 's sacking , even though it occurred in a division not under his control . In its aftermath , the union house committee passed a vote of no confidence in David Anderson , whom it said had " consistently failed to protect our ABC 's independence or protect staff when they are attacked " . In a long email , Stevens urged staff to stay united and undertook to hold " listening sessions " and support " culturally safe newsrooms " . <p> He prided himself on a consensus-driven style of management . His door was always open . He was effusive in his praise of staff , sending regular " herograms " , which endeared him to some staff but others read as " saying what everyone wanted to hear " . His approach to editorial management was fastidious , nitpicky , sometimes nervous . Some senior journalists saw him as cautious to a fault . " He would n't make a hard decision , " says one . " It was risk management first and then let 's think about doing some news after that , " comments another . A third says : " People are sad he 's gone because he supported a lot of people . But people want a boss who 's going to make uncomfortable decisions as well . " <p> Colleagues say Stevens seemed to age 10 years in the job . In a speech to the Melbourne Press Club , he bemoaned the " toxic media environment " and relentless " agenda-driven " attacks on the ABC and its journalists , which he called " bullying " . He stood up to those attacks . But he was lambasted for his handling of complaints against political reporter Laura Tingle after a session at the 2024 Sydney Writers ' Festival where she called Australia " a racist country " and criticised then opposition leader Peter Dutton 's rhetoric on migration . After a tirade from conservative commentators , Stevens deemed that Tingle 's comments did not meet the ABC 's editorial standards and said he 'd " counselled " her . ABC stalwart Quentin Dempster told The Saturday Paper , " Stevens has done what many ABC managers before him have done . By trying to appease the Murdoch attack dogs , they make them even more aggressive . " <p> Stevens ' former boss and mentor Anderson remains a staunch supporter . He told The Monthly : " I stand by my appointment of Justin Stevens . I think he was an excellent director of news for that period . I have no reservations about that whatsoever . None . " Stevens declined to comment for this story . <p> In 2024 , a fancy new broom swept into the ABC in the person of Kimberley Lynton Williams AM , appointed by the Albanese government to replace Ita Buttrose as chair . The prime minister announced : " Kim is such a perfect fit for the role , it 's almost as if he were made for it . " Williams has had an illustrious and tumultuous career . He 's headed the Australian Film Commission , Fox Studios , the Sydney Opera House Trust and Musica Viva . He rescued a floundering Foxtel in 2001 and over 10 years transformed it into a seriously profitable concern . He was hired by Rupert Murdoch as chief executive of News Limited in 2011 to overhaul its Australian operation , but bitterly clashed with Murdoch 's editors over cost cutting , and famously fell out with Murdoch 's son Lachlan in an explosive bust-up over NRL broadcast rights . <p> Williams is a classically trained clarinettist and composer of concertos . He was a junior Lego champion and had his own segment on a kids ' TV show at the age of 11 . His first wife was the novelist Kathy Lette and he is now married to public policy expert Catherine Dovey , the daughter of Gough and Margaret Whitlam . Williams is described as brilliant , highly strategic , kind , loyal , caustic , arrogant , pompous , condescending and terrifying . Published profiles over the years have called him a managerial genius , a good hater , a maker and destroyer , and a micromanaging control freak . He is accustomed to being the smartest person in any room . He has no qualms about voicing his opinions , often laced with expletives , and does n't suffer anyone or anything even remotely foolish . He 's been known to fly into temper tantrums described as " volcanic " by those who 've witnessed them . <p> Willams has always wanted to run the ABC . He applied for the role of managing director twice , the first time when he was 30 years old . Now 74 , he is a workaholic who spends more time in his ABC office than any chair in memory . He is a tireless advocate for the broadcaster . A devotee of Radio National , one of his first acts as chair was to kill a proposal by the previous board to get rid of the network . Williams successfully lobbied the government to increase the ABC 's funding by about $40 million a year . " That was Kim at his best , " a senior manager remarks . Subsequently , the government provided another $50 million to be spent on Australian content . <p> Williams is a voracious consumer of ABC news . He watches every current affairs show and has the ABC News channel on in his office all day . A former ABC board member said he has " a permanent love affair " with the national broadcaster . He can also be one of its most scathing critics if he thinks its standards have slipped . <p> Kim Williams and Justin Stevens were never really on the same page . Williams is a Homerophile who uses words such as apotheosis in casual conversation . Stevens is a nerdy nice guy , polite and self-deprecating , a bit of a dag who tells dad jokes and makes up affectionate nicknames for colleagues . Williams is a visionary in a hurry to reform the ABC ; Stevens is a stickler for process and protocol , who saw no need for urgent change . <p> Williams let it be known he regarded Stevens as promising but still not ready for the job . Says a well-placed insider : " He would often say ' How 's Justin going ? He 's very young . He 's got a lot of growing to do . ' " ( Never mind that Williams himself was only 30 when he applied to be managing director . ) Williams felt Stevens needed a mentor . The names of Kerry O'Brien and Norman Swan were mentioned . Stevens " did not avail himself " of the offer , says someone who was involved . " It was a vote of no confidence , " says another . <p> Williams makes it crystal clear when ABC content does not meet his uncompromising standards . " Kim is relentlessly unhappy with the news output , " says a senior program maker . In a meeting with Radio National staff , he complained there were not enough serious news items on the website , and too many lifestyle and sport stories -- a reasonable observation , which nonetheless irked . He was furious when an ABC news report incorrectly named the prime minister of Papua New Guinea in a story to mark the country 's 50th year of independence . He has described the ABC 's output as " bland " . An enthusiast for artificial intelligence ( he uses it to organise his emails ) , Williams was heard to say in one meeting that " AI would do a better job of writing news headlines than we do " , and in another that the ABC " would n't know how to spell documentary " . <p> Insiders say Williams was appalled by the news division 's handling of the Heston Russell stories and the subsequent court case , in which ABC witnesses were described by the judge as obdurate and combative . He believed the ABC 's international coverage was not up to scratch , and that its internal culture was too inward-looking and defensive . He thought diversity had probably gone too far . He certainly did not condone the hiring of a former outlaw motorcycle gang office-holder as a reporter on the storied current affairs flagship , Four Corners . <p> Williams would not be interviewed for this story , but provided written comments : " The ABC is the news , current affairs and documentary backbone of Australia . The nation would be immeasurably poorer without the range , scope and quality of those services . I do n't do public scorecards on their strengths and weaknesses other than to say we have the most substantial body of editorial policies of any Australian media enterprise and strive to perform against them consistently " . <p> When David Anderson retired , Williams seized the opportunity to choose his successor as managing director . Reports at the time speculated that Williams was looking for a " yes-man " or " compliant ally " . What 's clear is that he wanted someone who shared his appetite for change . <p> In December 2024 , Williams announced the appointment of Hugh Marks , the former chief executive of Nine Entertainment . Marks is a former corporate lawyer and 30-year veteran of the commercial television and independent production world . He engineered Nine 's takeover of the Fairfax newspapers , transforming it from an $800 million TV network into a $5 billion-plus media empire . He previously ran Southern Star Group , which produced Big Brother and Deal or No Deal . At Nine he launched Married at First Sight . <p> " Hugh is a commercial wheeler-dealer dude , " says a senior ABC staffer . Says another : " He presents as an urbane lawyer type , but you do n't get to be CEO of Nine without rat cunning and having some mongrel in you . You have to be smart and ruthless and take no prisoners . " <p> His appointment to the top job at Aunty raised some eyebrows as Marks had abruptly quit Nine amid headlines about a " sex scandal " , after admitting to a consensual relationship with a woman on his executive team , Alexi Baker . They have since married and had a child . <p> Separately , an internal review of Nine 's work practices and culture , commissioned well after Marks ' departure , found the company had " a systemic issue with abuse of power and authority , bullying , discrimination and sexual harassment " . Marks has since said he was unaware of any such problems during his time as chief executive of Nine . <p> Marks is smooth , smart , genial and assured . He calls the ABC " the business " . He likes to brainstorm and makes decisions quickly . Despite his " Hollywood Hugh " nickname , he was regarded at Nine as a solid supporter of news and current affairs . He backed investigative journalism and strongly defended stories such as the expos ? on disgraced war hero Ben Roberts-Smith , who sued Nine in a marathon court case and lost . Since joining the ABC , Marks has proven robust in defending personalities who have come under attack including Sarah Ferguson , Patricia Karvelas , Laura Tingle and Grace Tame . <p> Marks and Williams are both steeped in a cutthroat corporate culture where if you 're deemed to be not performing you can expect to be marched out the door . " They 're both real Type A dudes , " says a journalist . Another suspects a " hero complex " at work : " They both want to be the individuals that rescued the ABC from mediocrity . They want to be seen as the person that turned it around . " Williams and Marks share a mission to ensure the ABC is distinctive , impactful and trusted . Both want it to be bolder and less defensive . They have different priorities . Williams is a devotee of news and current affairs , radio and podcasts , while Marks ' great love is television , especially drama and documentaries . <p> Whatever Williams intended , Marks was n't going to be anyone 's yes-man . And early in his tenure he was gifted an opportunity to show just who was boss . In April 2025 , the ABC 's Media Watch revealed Williams had repeatedly rung ABC radio producers to push them to give airtime to an old acquaintance of his , Sandy Gutman , aka the comedian Austen Tayshus . Media Watch called it a grave lapse of judgement . Marks put out a statement vowing to be " vigilant to ensure the proper delineation of responsibility between the board and management " . Williams called it " an enormously stern learning experience " . <p> " That was a gift to Hugh because it gave him a mechanism to put a wall between Kim and management , and to set clear expectations for Kim regarding what was and was n't appropriate , " says a seasoned staffer . Or as someone else put it , " Kim had his knuckles rapped and he apologised and got back in his box . " Marks has since found cause to admonish Williams again after he called Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu an " aberrant creature " . Marks told Senate estimates that Williams ' comments were " less than desired or ideal " . <p> Marks moved quickly to flex his muscles in his new role as editor-in-chief . News boss Justin Stevens was instructed to ditch the " digital first " strategy he had championed and replace it with a new slogan : " audience first " ( the precise meaning of which continues to mystify staff ) . Marks ordered renewed focus on the 7pm nightly TV news , which he felt had been de-prioritised . He rejected the prevailing wisdom that free-to-air television was on its deathbed and set ambitious new targets for nightly programs , seen by some as " ridiculous " . <p> A program maker recalls : " Justin said upfront , ' I 'm under enormous pressure from Hugh . He really wants big broadcast ratings and you have to deliver them . ' " Another producer remembers Stevens arriving at meetings with a notebook in which he 'd written down Marks ' latest instructions . " Justin was constantly quoting Hugh -- it was clear he was trying to keep him happy . I was really struck by how obviously deferential he was to Hugh in all these meetings . He did n't want to put a word out of place . " <p> In June 2025 , Marks moved to axe the long-running discussion show Q+A , as part of a new restructure , with the loss of 40 jobs . The announcement was made by Stevens , who seemed to be singing from the same song sheet , saying , " We always need to keep innovating and renewing and , in the two decades since Q+A began , the world has changed . " <p> Marks reassigned Q+A 's budget , plus savings scraped from across the network ( totalling about $25 million ) , into a new " premium content fund " , which he controls , and a new documentary unit headed by former Australian Story executive producer Caitlin Shea , which staff call " Hugh 's baby " . Its first project was the 90-minute documentary " Diabolical " , on American sex offender Jeffrey Epstein , which ran in the Four Corners timeslot in March . Marks also commissioned a new talk show ( not unlike Q+A ) called ABC National Forum , hosted by David Speers , which also debuted in the Four Corners timeslot . <p> Marks is a champion of the independent production sector . He co-founded and ran his own production company , Dreamchaser Entertainment , after leaving Nine and believes more ABC content should be made externally and less in-house . He told Senate estimates when he was running Nine in 2016 that he thought the ABC 's production budget was too high and was crowding out its commercial rivals . He has been heard to say more recently that ABC News is bloated and over-resourced . The second project from his documentary unit was the two-part special " Deep Water " , on former Olympic swimmer turned drug trafficker Scott Miller , which ran in Australian Story 's timeslot in June . It was produced externally by Jam TV , owned by Marks ' old mate from Nine , Eddie McGuire . Asked if that suggests a conflict of interest , Marks told The Monthly : " I know pretty much every independent producer in the country . I 've worked with them for 30 years . If you regard colleagues and friends as a conflict , I have heaps . " <p> Some see the managing director 's " pet projects " as undermining established programs such as Four Corners and Australian Story , and the ABC 's own documentary department , by covering subjects in their remit and muscling in on their timeslots . " Hugh Marks says he wants to do ' less but better ' , " says a program-maker . " But the first thing he 's done was to use the Q+A money to fund a doco unit in competition with Four Corners and the investigative team . It 's ' more but worse ' . What he 's saying is the complete opposite of what he 's doing . " <p> Marks takes a keen interest in the ABC 's coverage of major events . On election night in May last year , after sitting in for the studio rehearsal , he was watching the ABC 's coverage at home as the results unfolded . It was the last hurrah for the ABC 's resident psephologist Antony Green , but it was painful to watch as his much-vaunted results computer malfunctioned throughout the night . The evening was a ratings winner for the ABC but was criticised afterwards as a victory of style ( snazzy studio , lots of high-profile commentators ) over substance . One of the ABC 's own panellists , former Liberal Party official turned pollster Tony Barry , derided it as " unwatchable drivel " , and declared the ABC should be burnt to the ground . A senior staffer called it a " wankfest " . The criticisms would not have gone unremarked in the executive suite . <p> All the while , another storm was building in Justin Stevens ' news division , around Four Corners reporter Mahmood Fazal . It 's a long and involved saga with far-reaching ramifications , so let 's start at the beginning . <p> Fazal had been an unlikely hire for the country 's most prestigious current affairs show . He was a former sergeant-at-arms of the Mongols outlaw motorcycle club , a self-confessed one-time meth addict with a history of violence . The son of Afghan refugees , Fazal grew up in Dandenong , in Melbourne 's south-east , joined a local gang and drifted into drug dealing and petty crime . He has spoken extensively about his previous life in podcasts . In one he said : " We used violence in a very aggressive way , and we got respect for it and people did n't fuck with us ... We began to use violence that got results and that intoxicated us further . " Aged about 20 , Fazal joined The Finks outlaw motorcycle gang . He later explained : " There was a guy in prison who was kind of a father figure to the Muslims in jail , who was very influential , and he was being patched into an outlaw motorcycle club . I was part of a group that were representing him on the outside , so we eventually became members . " <p> Fazal 's uncle was an underworld figure named Mohammed Akbar Keshtiar , known as Afghan Ali , who was serving 15 years in prison for attempted murder , after shooting a security guard at a Melbourne nightclub , and separately shooting his girlfriend and a friend in her home . Keshtiar was described as a recruiter for the Mongols , a United States -- based bikie gang that arrived in Australia in 2013 . The US Department of Justice lists the Mongols as an outlaw motorcycle gang whose members use their clubs as " conduits for criminal enterprises " such as " violent crime , weapons trafficking and drug trafficking " . The Finks amalgamated with the Mongols sometime around 2013 . <p> Fazal relished the bikie gang lifestyle . In one podcast he said " There were Maseratis in the carpark . We thought , fuck , this was glamorous . Two dozen strippers in the clubhouse . A lifestyle of excess . " In others he said , " It was actually a fuckin ' sick time , bro ... You feel like a rockstar . " <p> Fazal has been less vocal about what he actually did as the Mongols ' sergeant-at-arms , a role described on bikie gang websites as an " enforcer " . He has alluded to shocking violence and said he " did some heavy things " . " If you fuck up , you 're gon na get shot , you 're gon na get killed , that 's just the way it is , " he has said . Rule number one was that what happens in the club stays in the club . " We do n't talk to police . That 's the point of the code . You know , this omerta thing , " he said , referring to the mafia code of silence . After six years and the murder of several close friends , he decided to get out . <p> After leaving the Mongols , Fazal -- who had studied film at art school -- set out to reinvent himself as a documentary maker and journalist , specialising in crime . He said in 2020 : " People have written about crime from the outside for so long , but nobody 's written about crime from the inside and given criminals a platform to voice their opinion , and that 's by and large the main reason I wanted to be a journalist . " When he pitched a documentary about Islamic extremists to the US-based cable channel Vice TV , the project fell through , but he filed a story and Vice offered him a regular gig . <p> " They said , ' do you want a column ? ' " he later recounted . " I did n't even know what a column was ... I still ca n't fuckin ' believe it that these guys gave me a job . " He went on to write for Rolling Stone , Guardian Australia , The Sydney Morning Herald , The Monthly and The Saturday Paper . He made a documentary about the Japanese yakuza and interviewed Osama bin Laden 's son . He became a gourmand and an art buff , wrote art reviews and food reviews , and a wine column for the men 's magazine Esquire . He bottled his own shiraz . A former colleague calls him a " renaissance man " . He made an award-winning podcast called No Gangsters in Paradise . He was feted for his " incredible story of redemption " . <p> In 2021 , Fazal was hired by the ABC to work for the investigative radio show Background Briefing . He filed stories on abuse in youth detention , drugs in prison , the perils of sex work , and convicted terrorists eager for the platform he could provide . <p> Then , in 2022 , he was seconded to Four Corners to help with a story about the so-called postcode wars , between rival crime gangs in Western Sydney . Fazal gave the program an entree to the infamous Alameddine crime family . During an interview aired on Four Corners , an associate of the crime gang can be seen on the sidelines instructing an interviewee on what to say . " Make it very clear that the Alameddine organised crime network does not exist . It 's a police-made thing , " says the associate . The " talent " replies to camera , " Yeah , there 's no criminal network . There 's nothing like that . That 's something the police have made up or the media have made up . " <p> Some of the Four Corners team were concerned about Fazal 's way of working . He was guarded and defensive about his sources , and sometimes would n't provide their real names . His information was hard to fact-check . He seemed overly friendly with the gangsters he was reporting on . Former staff say that when they raised these concerns with management , they were brushed off . <p> In 2023 , Fazal was hired as a reporter on Four Corners , perhaps the most prestigious job in journalism . Its executive producer at the time was Matthew Carney and his deputy was Alice Brennan , who had initially recruited Fazal into the ABC . " He was welcomed with open arms , " says a former colleague . " All his dreams had come true , " says another . Fazal delivered a suite of stories on the cocaine industry , the meth trade , undercover operatives and gangland hits . His stories featured interviews with anonymous drug dealers , hit men , and gangsters wearing masks and disguises . Insiders say Fazal 's superiors were thrilled by his " sexy " stories . <p> In August 2023 , while Fazal was filming a story for Four Corners on undercover operatives , his uncle Mohammed Keshtiar was shot dead in a gangland hit in Melbourne 's well-heeled South Yarra . Fazal was said by colleagues to be devastated by his uncle 's death . Police posted a $1 million reward for information about the murder and said they were investigating links to outlaw motorcycle gangs , Middle Eastern crime syndicates and the city 's " tobacco war " , in which scores of illicit tobacco shops had been torched . <p> Within the ABC , serious concerns about Fazal 's modus operandi persisted . Much of the time , Fazal worked remotely from his rural property in Victoria with minimal supervision . Sometimes the producers working with him did n't know who his anonymous contacts were . Some of his information could n't be corroborated -- they had to take his word . Says one insider : " There was no process for verifying confidential sources . It makes me feel sick . " <p> " I was worried , " says a former Four Corners staffer . " I raised it over and over again . He was not equipped for the job , he was not trained properly , he was not supported properly . " <p> " People found it difficult to criticise his work , " says another , " maybe out of concern they 'd be seen as not supporting a young brown man who 'd made it to Four Corners . It was a failure by multiple levels of management . " <p> It was " a car crash " , comments a third . <p> They got the impression management did n't want to know . " It became clear it was not good for my career to keep pushing it , " says a fourth . <p> The concerns were not confined to Four Corners . An ABC executive who saw Fazal 's reports as glorifying drug dealers raised it with Fazal 's superiors . " He 's bringing in good stories " , was the response . Another journalist was so troubled they raised it with police , who had their own issues with criminals being given such a prestigious platform . A senior police officer told The Monthly : " Their persona online becomes bigger than them and it attracts younger people into that milieu ... It generates hits for them online . It enhances the gangster style . It does n't report the facts , it 's just propaganda . " <p> The concerns about Fazal erupted publicly in May 2024 , when the Nine newspapers ran a report by investigative journalist Kate McClymont , which said that Fazal had passed on threats from the Alameddine crime syndicate to a producer working for YouTuber Jordan Shanks , known as friendlyjordies . Shanks had angered the syndicate by posting a video that featured photographs of some of its members . The photos had been supplied courtesy of Fazal , who had previously run them in an article for Rolling Stone . Shanks ' house in Bondi was firebombed in November 2022 , apparently in retaliation for the video , and an associate of the crime family was later jailed for five years over the attack . But Shanks refused to take the video offline . <p> In January 2024 , Shanks ' producer , Kristo Langker , made a statement to NSW Police -- reported by McClymont -- about a meeting he had with Fazal earlier that month . Langker told police that Fazal had contacted him to say he had people threatening to kill him if the video was n't taken down . According to Langker 's police statement : " Fazal said words to the effect that ' if the video did n't come down something very bad could happen to himself ' . He made it very apparent that Jordan and I were also in danger . Mahmood said ' Jordan has already been firebombed . What more could it take to take the video down ? ' " Langker called his lawyer , Mark Davis , and the two of them met up with Fazal in Sydney . Langker and Davis say Fazal told them he had just come from a meeting with an Alameddine associate . Langker 's statement records : " Mahmood said ' These people kill people . If you do n't take the video down something bad is going to happen . ' " <p> Davis recalls Fazal mentioning that the people in question " re-arrange people 's faces " . Davis tells The Monthly , " It was very sinister . There 's no doubt in my mind -- unquestionably he was there brazenly passing on a death threat . " Davis says he offered to act for Fazal and go to the police with him , but Fazal declined . Langker said when he told Fazal later he was going to the police , Fazal replied , " Do you know what happens to people that do that ? " Langker added that when he told Fazal he believed he was being threatened , Fazal said , " I am the one being threatened here , mate , not you . " <p> Mahmood Fazal did not reply to repeated requests for comment from The Monthly . At the time , he denied the Kate McClymont story . In a statement , he told The Sydney Morning Herald : " I have not engaged in any unlawful conduct . The connections you seek to draw ... are serious and profoundly offensive ... This very much appears to be a case of attempting to stereotype me . Much of what you have asserted relies on what can only be described as rank speculation and untenable reasoning . " <p> The ABC rushed to back Fazal , asserting in a statement : " Mahmood Fazal does extremely challenging , impactful and important public interest journalism ... and the ABC stands by his reporting . " The same day , Four Corners released a statement saying : " The Four Corners Staff -- reporters , producers , researchers , camera operators , editors , graphic artists , digital producers , the program 's supervising producer and its executive producer -- stand behind Mahmood Fazal and his investigative journalism . Mahmood is an award-winning , ethical and fact-based reporter of the highest standard . The entire team rejects any suggestion to the contrary . " But in fact , some of the team were " aghast " that this unqualified endorsement had been provided on their behalf , without the allegations having been examined or tested . <p> Langker wrote to the ABC -- including to Justin Stevens -- to complain about its failure to investigate the allegations about Fazal . Langker received a reply saying , " We have no evidence of any illegality or misconduct by an ABC employee . If you have any evidence ... please report it to the authorities . " Langker wrote to Stevens again , saying he and Shanks still feared for their safety . Stevens responded , six weeks later : " I am sorry to hear about your concerns for your own and others ' safety ... I can only reiterate that if you have any evidence of illegality or concerns for your safety , the appropriate place to report it ... is to the police . " <p> Davis , himself a former Four Corners reporter , was stunned by the ABC 's response . " I assumed the ABC would be horrified . I was astounded the ABC did n't do anything , absolutely astounded . They did n't even blink . " <p> In October 2025 , Fazal was in the news again when The Sydney Morning Herald filed another story revealing the ABC was investigating him over a podcast he 'd launched called Word on the Street , billed as " the ultimate insider 's guide to Australia 's underworld and gang crime scenes " . The story revealed Fazal had fallen out with his co-host , former criminal turned " media cowboy " , Ryan Naumenko . A furious Naumenko had then gone public , claiming Fazal had demanded payment in cash as he had n't told the ABC he was being paid for the podcast . Naumenko also claimed Fazal had proposed an episode on the Alameddine crime family " to earn some respect " from them . In a statement , the ABC said Fazal 's podcast had initially been endorsed by his manager , but that endorsement was withdrawn after it went to air with gambling ads . <p> A few days later , Media Watch revealed purported text messages between Naumenko and Fazal , in which Fazal explained he wanted cash because he did n't " want to trip up work stuff if I get caught being paid " . Fazal 's lawyer , Rebekah Giles , released a statement saying , " Mr Fazal agreed to be interviewed by Mr Naumenko believing he had his manager 's approval ... The allegations now being peddled by Mr Naumenko are categorically denied by Mr Fazal . " The ABC said it was now " thoroughly investigating this matter " . <p> But the story would n't go away . The same week , The Sydney Morning Herald reported another series of text messages between Naumenko and Fazal . The exchange included messages purportedly from Fazal : " Fuck Jordies and his people they 're dogs " , " I do n't know why he s crying ... comes with the territory ... u want to shit on gangsters and make fun of them in ur video " , " he has to get what he gets he 's a dog ratted me to jacks . " Another message said : " Bro Alameddines still wan na hit me I need to make up to em with this podcast . " <p> The Sydney Morning Herald reported that one message expressed a wish to harm Shanks , wording Fazal said was falsified . The other messages , Fazal claimed , were " taken out of context " . He told The Sydney Morning Herald , " You have put a fabricated message to me and select sic messages out of context . I deny any wrongdoing . " Shortly after this , Fazal went on extended leave from the ABC . <p> By late last year , questions were being asked about whether the anonymous gangsters in some of Fazal 's Four Corners reporting were who they purported to be . Concerns were raised by colleagues about two of his stories , " Cocaine Nation " , which went to air in May 2023 , and " Meth Highway " , which aired in April 2024 , both of which featured numerous drug dealers and other criminals whose identities were disguised . Journalists who had worked with Fazal told a senior ABC manager they had serious concerns about the true identities of at least two of the anonymous characters featured in these reports . Those concerns have since been borne out . It is now known that an associate of Fazal played different roles in different stories , disguised behind a mask . The ABC is now understood to be reviewing all of Fazal 's stories . <p> In November , Four Corners executive producer Matthew Carney resigned after a serious illness . Justin Stevens told staff Carney 's departure was unrelated to Fazal , and Carney echoed this . Regarding Fazal , Carney said in a statement " Mahmood Fazal 's stories were all subjected to the Four Corners ' rigorous fact check , verification and legal processes . The identities of anonymous interviewees were independently verified with multiple sources before they were published . Those verifications came from policing and law enforcement bodies , as well as court records and court evidence such as phone taps . " <p> Carney was replaced in March by Joel Tozer , the former executive producer of 7.30 . In an email to staff in May , Tozer said : " Since taking on this position , I have been reviewing our editorial , commissioning and development processes , including verification , legal and editorial risk management , and have identified some gaps ... It has become evident that the current structure does not provide enough dedicated oversight and consistency to ensure effective management and controls " . Tozer revealed his deputy , Alice Brennan , was being made redundant and he was creating a new role of " editor , commissioning and standards " to oversee research , story verification and editorial processes . <p> Mahmood Fazal did not reply to repeated approaches for comment from The Monthly . In response to written questions , his lawyer Michael Bradley responded , " I do n't have anything for you . " <p> An ABC spokesperson said , " The ABC is aware Mahmood Fazal has filed an application with the Fair Work Commission . As the matter is currently before the commission , it would be inappropriate for the ABC to comment further . " <p> Hugh Marks and Kim Williams declined to answer questions about Fazal . Insiders say they were both appalled by how he had been managed and the ensuing reputational damage to the ABC . <p> In late November , Hugh Marks and Justin Stevens sat side by side at the annual Walkley Awards gala dinner in Sydney , toasting their success . On the face of it , Stevens had reason to smile . He 'd just got a $100,000 pay rise , taking his salary to $667,900 . It was a festive night for the ABC , which won nine awards , including the Gold Walkley for Adele Ferguson and Chris Gillett 's expos ? s on failings in childcare . A few days later , Marks told Senate estimates it had been a big year for the ABC . Its trust rating was at 80 per cent . Its digital news service was ensconced at number one . Audio ratings were up 7 per cent , with 50 podcasts in Australia 's top 300 . It had won a record 11 Logie Awards . <p> But behind the public smiles , Stevens ' days were numbered . Four Corners was in damage control over the Fazal affair . The ABC 's news coverage of the Bondi shooting tragedy in December was seen as slow and inferior to its commercial rivals . Marks and Stevens disagreed on subject matter for the new documentary unit , with Stevens reacting angrily when a project he had n't approved was green-lit by Marks . At the same time , Stevens had to manage a nasty falling-out between two senior members of staff . Marks wanted Stevens to sack a member of his news executive team , but he refused . Stevens ' relations with his bosses were at breaking point , yet he seemingly had no idea moves were already under way to replace him . " Scared is too strong a word , but he was on tenterhooks to survive , " says one insider . <p> By early in the new year , Marks and Kim Williams had identified the man they wanted for the job : Simon Robinson , the Australian-born , London-based deputy to the editor-in-chief of international news service , Reuters . Robinson is well known to Williams who , until January , chaired the Thomson Reuters Founders Share Company , whose role is safeguarding the news service 's principles and independence . A highly regarded former correspondent and editorial leader , Robinson will be the first outsider to head ABC News in 30 years . <p> On May 21 , Stevens was called in to a meeting with Marks and was told Robinson had been hired to replace him . Marks hoped Stevens would go quietly and asked him to stay until September , when Robinson is due to start . Stevens declined the offer , went home and six days later sent his " I have resigned " email to staff . <p> Marks has thus far declined to say exactly why he got rid of Stevens . He told The Monthly , " Justin gave 100 per cent to this place . He ca n't be criticised on that basis . " Then came the " but " . " We 've rested on our laurels in terms of news content . What have we done that 's new in the last decade ? " <p> Pressed for a clearer explanation , Marks said : " There were two really important things on which we disagreed . The first was -- what are the new news formats we can explore and offer in a way that 's useful to the audience ? I 've been pushing that for a while . How many new ideas have we come up with ? None . Second -- are we capable of making the decisions and providing the clarity and leadership in regard to what might need to change ? Every show is under budgeted . There has to be a change if that 's not going to break . " <p> In the absence of clear reasons , ABC staff have been left to explain among themselves why Stevens was pushed . The powers-that-be just did n't like him . He would n't toe their line . His style was anathema to theirs . They saw his softness as weakness . There had been too many missteps , too many errors of judgement . He did n't have a vision . They genuinely believed he was n't up to the job . They needed someone to swing the axe and Stevens was n't the guy . <p> Marks has hinted at major change to come . " It 's not a time for incrementally adjusting the dials , " he says . In May , he set off frenzied speculation when he commented in an interview : " I 'll give you an example . A legacy television show that has been around for 40 years ... that particular show may not be the sort of content we want for the future . " The remark sent program-makers across the country into a spin over whose baby was for the chop . Presenter Sarah Ferguson asked Marks for an assurance that the program he had in mind was not her show , 7.30 , whose future has been the subject of much speculation over the years . Ferguson got the assurance she wanted . Marks has since dismissed his comment as " a Hugh fuck-up " . <p> " Hugh likes to throw bombs out there and see how they land , " says an old ABC hand . " He likes to keep people off balance . He quite likes people not being sure what he 's going to do . " <p> Once again , as at so many junctures in its history , the ABC is in a state of flux . Major change is afoot . No one -- except perhaps Hugh Marks and Kim Williams -- knows what it will entail . <p> " It 's dawning on people that it was done for a particular reason -- they want change , " says a senior journalist . " We 're waiting for the night of the long knives , " says another . " That 's the mood . What did Paul Keating say ? ' Everybody leaves here in a box . ' " <p> Sally Neighbour worked for the ABC for 30 years as a reporter , investigative journalist , correspondent and executive producer of Four Corners ( 2015 -- 22 ) and 7.30 ( 2012 -- 15 ) . She has won four Walkley Awards . <h> Read more on : <p> Sally Neighbour worked for the ABC for 30 years as a reporter , investigative journalist , correspondent and executive producer of Four Corners ( 2015 -- 22 ) and 7.30 ( 2012 -- 15 ) . She has won four Walkley Awards .

@@139294280 <p> Journalists spent a lot of last week 's Labor Party conference lamenting the absence of heated debates on the conference floor . <p> A bit of biffo over issues like same sex marriage or boat turn-backs has been a lightning-rod moment in recent years , as the ALP has sought to settle divisive debates that have big implications for the party 's ability to be competitive at elections . <p> It is easy to see why the media , hanging on the sidelines of Labor 's three-day camp , were frustrated they did n't have a fiery stoush to help fill air time and column inches . For Labor , the more lasting issue is what sacrifice comes with its command and control culture . <p> Much has been said about Labor 's rigid discipline under Anthony Albanese . It comes as little surprise to party insiders who often joke about the fact that the prime minister , who came through the ranks of the NSW left faction , managed to end up not only in charge of the left , but also exerting control over its rival , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , gambling reforms , AUKUS and the Middle East were all settled behind closed doors . The only public pickle for the PM came courtesy of an errant escalator . <p> That is aside from former minister Ed Husic , who spent much of the lead-up to the conference arguing the Labor caucus was effectively gagged from offering any view that contradicted sanctioned government talking points . <p> His attempt to amend the party platform to take a tougher line on Israel in relation to the conflict in Gaza and the West Bank failed on Saturday , after the Maritime Union withdrew its support . Husic hit out at his colleagues and said he was " surprised " at the energy applied to shut down his proposal . <p> Senior Labor figures rejected his claims and said that Husic ultimately could n't muster enough support for his resolution . <p> They point to the fact that the party adopted a position that was supported by Victorian MPs Josh Burns , who is Jewish , and Basem Abdo , the son of Palestinian refugees . Both appeared on stage and spoke @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ with the official government position . <p> But Husic warned that a party that does n't allow democracy to flourish loses connection with voters . <h> A broader point at play <p> He has a broader point , but it is worth noting the conference is not a town hall public policy forum , but a rank-and-file meeting of Labor members , delegates , unions and politicians . <p> And Albanese is not the only leader to have an iron grip on proceedings . <p> For those nostalgic about conferences past , Bob Hawke delivered a cracker of a show in 1990 . <p> Hawke sprung a surprise conference on the party to effectively rubber stamp a cabinet decision to privatise Australian Airlines and part of Qantas . <p> There is a key difference . Hawke brought on the conference , but he also opened the floor to give opponents to privatisation an opportunity to make their case . Hawke won the day , but members had the chance to argue the toss . <p> In shutting down Husic 's proposed amendment , Albanese risks sending a broader message that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ genuine contest of ideas , with real decisions made behind closed doors . <p> That goes back to Husic 's point about Labor closing down disagreement . <p> One Nation has become a place for voters to go who feel like the major parties are n't listening to them . But the reason it 's gained popularity is hardly that its internal mechanisms are filled with democratic expression . <p> One Nation is Pauline Hanson 's baby , and unlike parties of the centre , it does n't have to settle debates about whether it should support Muslim immigration or not -- it hardly has to balance another flank of opinion . It 's Hanson 's way or the highway . <p> Voters are drawn to One Nation because Hanson has modelled herself as the antithesis of mainstream politicians , less so because of a robust internal policy process . Yet the risk for Albanese is that what critics argue is him stifling conference debate bleeds into how he registers voter complaints . <p> Let 's look at the counterfactual . Imagine a conference with fierce debates on AUKUS , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Now imagine how that would be weaponised against the government . <p> Under Albanese , Labor has been obsessive in avoiding handing its opponents a victory on culture wars and being accused of focusing on niche issues . Albanese believes that being a party of government means parking topics that do n't align with mainstream issues like the cost of living . <p> Labor figures will tell you they will gladly cop the media condemning their lack of public debate and democracy . They care about what voters see . And in their assessment , voters not engaged with the daily rhythm of politics see a party that is willing to put aside its differences to pursue its overwhelming aims . <p> The Liberals ' electoral headache has coincided with its own lack of discipline and a growing obsession with issues that are out of whack with mainstream Australia . Debating immigration is fair game in a country that feels under pressure . Flirting with the idea that multiculturalism has failed is much sharper and risks alienating scores of voters for whom multiculturalism is their reality . <h> Cosplaying unity @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the display of unity at conference was a deliberate contrast to the Liberal Party , which he painted as deeply fractured and fed into a broader theme of the conference -- linking the Liberal Party to One Nation . <p> You can understand the bigger play . If your entire political messaging and strategy is to paint the opposition as bitterly divided , you need to cosplay extreme unity . <p> The ABC 's politics podcast , Politics Now , dives into the biggest stories , giving you a balanced look inside the strategies and decisions behind the headlines . <p> That strategy has so far worked for Albanese ; he has won two elections , the last securing a historic number of Labor seats . He will no doubt welcome coverage of the conference being dull and stage-managed . <p> The PM said as much on ABC yesterday . Appearing on Insiders , he offered this : " We are more cohesive , more united than we 've ever been before . And I think that 's a good thing . " <p> Where Hawke stitched up the numbers @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's ruthless control risks looking like taking the win and not bothering with the debate .

@@139376474 <p> Zombies , ghosts , saints -- the 2026 Miles Franklin Literary Award shortlist is not short on colour . <p> The prestigious writing prize has been won ( more than once ) by the likes of Tim Winton , Thea Astley and Michelle de Kretser . <p> This year 's shortlist takes us from the forests of Malaysian Borneo to colonial-era Tasmania , with some surprising and supernatural diversions along the way . <p> It 's a year of slender books , with Josephine Rowe 's novella Little World coming in at just 130 pages and Sean Wilson 's sophomore novel , You Must Remember This , at 176 . <p> The six finalists share preoccupations with queerness , nature and the macabre , and many illuminate previously overlooked corners of Australian history . <p> It 's not all about the past -- Discipline by Randa Abdel-Fattah , whose cancelled appearance at Adelaide Writers ' Week led to a furore resulting in the axing of the 2026 event , grapples with some of the most divisive political issues of the present moment . <p> ABC Radio National 's The @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ one of Australia 's most prestigious literary awards . <p> Two of the titles are debuts : My Heart at Evening by **39;0;TOOLONG Konrad Muller and First Name Second Name by fellow multi-hyphenate Steve MinOn , a former advertising copywriter and restaurateur . <p> To take a closer look at the shortlist , we 've brought together The Book Show 's Claire Nichols and Sarah L'Estrange , The Bookshelf 's Kate Evans and writer Declan Fry . <h> The overall vibe <p> CN : This is a very fun and readable shortlist . The quality is very high and it 's nice to have a list where there are n't a lot of ' worthy ' books -- books that make you feel like you 're doing a lot of work while you 're reading them . They 're very enjoyable . <p> DF : I could n't disagree more ! I felt these books did ask for work from readers . That 's what makes them special . The approaches to queer characterisation in My Heart at Evening , Fierceland , Little World and First Name Second Name are @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's shortlist appear straightforward but they 're incredibly polyvocal ; they say so many things at once . They 're really complex . <p> SL : I was pleasantly surprised going into the list . Konrad Muller 's My Heart at Evening is the first book published by micro press Evercreech Editions -- I did n't know about this title and then opened this beautifully designed book and discovered another world . It 's got the pathos and gothic noir that we associate with Tasmanian colonial fiction but there 's also a Dickensian element as well . It had lots of surprises . I think each of the books have something to surprise . <p> KE : I was really taken by the way that so many of the titles are dealing with the past in such interesting and playful ways , offering a fresh take on historical fiction . <h> Discipline by Randa Abdel-Fattah <p> Abdel-Fattah is a sociologist and the author of several books for younger readers . ( Supplied : UQP ) <p> CN : Discipline is set in 2021 amidst a round of bombings in Gaza @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is charged with terrorism offences after protesting against a university 's connection to a weapons manufacturer in Israel . <p> We meet two Muslim Australians . One is an academic working in a university and the other is a journalist working for a newspaper . The story is about these two characters and how they respond to these incidents and what it means to speak out or not speak out against censorship . <p> KE : I found it immensely stressful as a reader , partly because of the way it took us into the domestic lives of these Australian characters , who I thought were really well delineated , and then put them in tension with this huge geopolitical situation . That 's the part of the book that worked the best for me . <p> CN : For me , the pain of having to deal with the domestic and the global at the same time was the strongest aspect of Discipline . It asks the reader to think about what it must be like to get up and be so terrified about what 's happening in your @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ safe , to be so worried , but also have to think about what is going on the table for dinner tonight ? How am I going to take care of my child today ? How 's the person in my work ignoring me ? <p> It 's a book with a message and it does n't hold back in delivering its message . <h> First Name Second Name by Steve MinOn <p> MinOn is the author of children 's books , including his latest , Make Them Say Poo . ( Supplied : UQP ) <p> KE : This is a novel where the main character wakes up dead . I rushed through the zombie bits because I wanted to get back to the family . <p> CN : I was also really moved by the stories from the past . I loved meeting the multiple generations of this family ; I think Steve MinOn does it so well . <p> The juxtaposition of that with this zombie who keeps losing bits of his body and attacking people and sucking the life out of them as he moves further north towards @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 'm always thrilled when Aussie authors just go for it with a really bonkers idea . I found this a very pleasurable read . <h> My Heart at Evening by Konrad Muller <p> DF : My Heart at Evening is set in 1832 in Van Diemen 's Land . A man is sent to learn what 's happened to Henry Hellyer , a real-life figure . We know how Hellyer died -- the question is why did he die ? Why did he kill himself ? Various people come and reveal their interpretations of what happened in this kind of Rashomon-style set-up . <p> One possibility is he committed suicide because he was gay , and the shame of being homosexual at that time makes it completely plausible . Or perhaps it was because of the violence and the brutalisation of colonisation . It could be all of these things or none of them -- that 's the heart of mystery and darkness in this very Joseph Conradian novel . <p> KE : I loved the voice of this novel . From the very first page , I thought this @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ addressing his beloved . It 's almost a second-person telling of a story that she will never receive -- it 's like a letter that is never sent . <p> And then there 's all of that rich colonial context which , as Sarah said , almost feels Dickensian . There 's a real 19th-century style to it and then overlaying it there 's all this really interesting convict history . There 's the Van Diemen 's Land Company , which really existed and played a role similar to the East India Company , so it 's not that you 've got a government who 's shaping this terrible , violent colonial moment ; you 've got corporate entities committing violence against the land and Aboriginal people . <p> Some of it 's almost happening at the edge of the main character 's eyesight . He ca n't even quite look at it . It 's so beautifully done . <p> CN : I love that this is essentially a detective novel . We 've got a mystery to solve ; we 're going to interview various people and figure @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's such an Australian novel in that our detective is a convict , and it 's a story that could only be set in this place . I think that 's really delicious . <p> SL : It 's not just a detective novel ; it draws on that classic trope of Agatha Christie of the locked-room mystery as well . It 's like a red herring , but it 's a delightful element within the story , which gives levity to the brooding elements in the book . <p> KE : And the rogues and the ratbags and the convict cant street slang that he plays around with as well . <p> CN : I love that everyone who gets interviewed are such specific characters .
